The Mixture of Ex<sub>p</sub>erts (MoE) architecture was introduced to scale model ca<sub>p</sub>acit<sub>y</sub> throu<sub>g</sub>h conditional com<sub>p</sub>utation [7, 10], and it is now widel<sub>y</sub> used to co<sub>p</sub>e with hetero<sub>g</sub>eneous data and com<sub>p</sub>etin<sub>g</sub> o<sub>p</sub>timization si<sub>g</sub>nals. ## 2.4 Disentangled Representation Learning & Curriculum Optimization

Be<sub>y</sub>ond holistic re<sub>p</sub>resentation learnin<sub>g</sub>, Disentan<sub>g</sub>led Re<sub>p</sub>resentation Learnin<sub>g</sub> (DRL) [31, 47] aims to decom<sub>p</sub>ose user <sub>p</sub>references into inde<sub>p</sub>endent latent factors<sub>,</sub> im<sub>p</sub>rovin<sub>g</sub> inter<sub>p</sub>retabilit<sub>y</sub> and robustness [48]. MacridVAE [33] and DGCF [51] establish this line b<sub>y</sub> factorizin<sub>g</sub> user intents into macro-conce<sub>p</sub>ts and fine-<sub>g</sub>rained <sub>p</sub>references<sub>,</sub> res<sub>p</sub>ectivel<sub>y</sub>. In multimodal recommendation<sub>,</sub> DRL is further used to se<sub>p</sub>arate modalit<sub>y</sub>-s<sub>p</sub>ecific interests from content-invariant <sub>p</sub>references. DMRL [28] a<sub>pp</sub>lies disentan<sub>g</sub>led attention to model users’ var<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> focus on diferent modalities, while DICER [69] and DCCF [39] incor<sub>p</sub>orate causal intervention and disentan<sub>g</sub>led contrastive learnin<sub>g</sub> to reduce s<sub>p</sub>urious correlations and confoundin<sub>g</sub> efects. A recurrin<sub>g</sub> dificult<sub>y</sub> is o<sub>p</sub>timiza tion: <sup>d</sup>isentang<sup>l</sup>ement o<sup>b</sup>jectives, especia<sup>ll</sup>y w<sup>h</sup>en com<sup>b</sup>ine<sup>d</sup> wit<sup>h</sup> expert-sty<sup>l</sup>e arc<sup>h</sup>itectures, can <sup>b</sup>e sensitive and <sub>p</sub>rone to instabilit<sub>y</sub> or colla<sub>p</sub>se without additional <sub>g</sub>uidance.

Curriculum Learnin<sub>g</sub> (CL) [49] <sub>p</sub>rovides a com<sub>p</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> trainin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>rinci<sub>p</sub>le b<sub>y</sub> schedulin<sub>g</sub> learnin<sub>g</sub> from easier si<sub>g</sub>nals to harder ones<sub>,</sub> which hel<sub>p</sub>s avoid <sub>p</sub>oor local o<sub>p</sub>tima. In se<sub>q</sub>uential recommendation, Dif4Rec [58] and CCL [2] <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressivel<sub>y</sub> increase au<sub>g</sub>mentation dificult<sub>y</sub> to learn robust <sub>p</sub>references from nois<sub>y</sub> behaviors. Similar ideas a<sub>pp</sub>ear in <sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>h-based learnin<sub>g</sub>: MixGCF [19] stren<sub>g</sub>thens discrimination via <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressivel<sub>y</sub> harder ne<sub>g</sub>atives, AdaGCL [21] adjusts contrastive views durin<sub>g</sub> trainin<sub>g</sub>, and CDR [3] combines curriculum schedulin<sub>g</sub> with disentan<sub>g</sub>lement under nois<sub>y</sub> multi-feedback. These findin<sub>g</sub>s su<sub>pp</sub>ort our desi<sub>g</sub>n: MAGNET cou<sub>p</sub>les ex<sub>p</sub>licit structural disentan<sub>g</sub>lement<sub>,</sub> im<sub>p</sub>lemented throu<sub>g</sub>h tri<sub>p</sub>let-tem<sub>p</sub>late ex<sub>p</sub>erts<sub>,</sub> with <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressive routin<sub>g</sub> so that multimodal fusion[18, 66] remains stable while retainin<sub>g</sub> inter<sub>p</sub>retabilit<sub>y</sub>.

Balancin<sub>g</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>loration and ex<sub>p</sub>loitation is central to trainin<sub>g</sub> s<sub>p</sub>arse neural architectures. Recent <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ress in A<sub>g</sub>entic AI [4] ofers a two-sta<sub>g</sub>e, entro<sub>py</sub>-aware o<sub>p</sub>timization <sub>p</sub>ers<sub>p</sub>ective that is directl<sub>y</sub> relevant to routin<sub>g</sub>. Fig. 2. Overview of our proposed MAGNET framework. The first row presents the end-to-end pipeline: (I) inputs user–item interactions and item-side visual/text features; (II) constructs content-induced edges via similarity and KNN retrieval to augment the graph; (III) performs dual-view encoding on observed and augmented views and fuses them into unified user/item representations; (IV) applies a routing-based sparse triplet MoE as the prediction head, routing each query to a sparse set of experts and aggregating their outputs under a unified training objective. The second row provides complementary details: (A) illustrates the triplet-template expert pool covering behavior/appearance/semantics paterns, and (B) shows the progressive entropy-guided routing schedule that transitions from exploration to specialization during training.

from raw item features. Followin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>rior work<sub>,</sub> we use cosine similarit<sub>y</sub>:

$$

## 3.3 Triplet-template Mixture-of-Experts for Multimodal Fusion

MAGNET tr<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>s</sub> int<sub>e</sub>r<sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n-ind<sub>uce</sub>d b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>o</sub>r <sub>as</sub> th<sub>e p</sub>rim<sub>a</sub>r<sub>y supe</sub>r<sub>v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>o</sub>n <sub>s</sub>i<sub>g</sub>n<sub>a</sub>l <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>uses</sub> it<sub>e</sub>m-<sub>s</sub>id<sub>e</sub> multimodal contents as com<sub>p</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> evidence. From the dual-view structural backbone (Section 3.2), we obtain fused behavior embeddin<sub>g</sub>s $z _ { u } , z _ { i } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ f<sub>or eac</sub>h i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en</sub> $( u , i )$ <sub>.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> th<sub>en</sub> build a structured Mixture-of-Ex<sub>p</sub>erts (MoE) la<sub>y</sub>er for ada<sub>p</sub>tive multimodal fusion: each ex<sub>p</sub>ert im<sub>p</sub>lements an inter<sub>p</sub>retable tri<sub>p</sub>let com<sub>p</sub>osition over $\{ B , A , S \}$ i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>an</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d f<sub>rom s</sub>h<sub>are</sub>d t<sub>emp</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es,</sub> while a router learns token-wise s<sub>p</sub>arse <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> over this structured ex<sub>p</sub>ert <sub>p</sub>ool. Im<sub>p</sub>ortantl<sub>y,</sub> our “<sub>mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>lit<sub>y-gu</sub>id<sub>e</sub>d” d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn</sub> <sub>comes</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>exper</sub>t <sub>space:</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>exper</sub>t f<sub>am</sub>ili<sub>es</sub> <sub>an</sub>d t<sub>emp</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>pre-</sub> defined to reflect modalit<sub>y</sub> roles, and an entro<sub>py</sub>-tri<sub>gg</sub>ered <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressive routin<sub>g</sub> scheme (Section 3.4) <sub>g</sub>raduall<sub>y</sub> relaxes routin<sub>g</sub> re<sub>g</sub>ularization durin<sub>g</sub> trainin<sub>g</sub>, reducin<sub>g</sub> manual intervention and allowin<sub>g</sub> the <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub> to become increasin<sub>g</sub>l<sub>y</sub> data-ada<sub>p</sub>tive.

<div class="mineru-algorithm" style="white-space: pre-wrap; font-family:monospace;">
Algorithm 1: MAGNET Scoring via Modality-Guided MoE (Sparse Expert Activation)

Require: Behavior embeddings  $z_{u}, z_{i}$ ; modality cues  $\{h_{u}^{m}\}_{m\in M}, \{h_{i}^{m}\}_{m\in M}$ ;
Router parameters  $\Theta_{rt}$ ; expert parameters  $\{\Theta_{e}\}_{e=1}^{E}$ ; activated experts K.

Ensure: Preference score  $\hat{y}_{ui}$ , dense routing  $\pi_{ui}$ , activated expert set  $\Gamma_{ui}$ , and renormalized weights  $\tilde{\pi}_{ui}$ .

2 Step 0: Prepare modality cues
3 if  $\{h_{u}^{m}\}, \{h_{i}^{m}\}$  are not precomputed then
4 Compute modality cues  $\{h_{u}^{m}\}_{m\in M}$  and  $\{h_{i}^{m}\}_{m\in M}$ 
5 end
6 Step 1: Dense routing distribution // for entropy &amp; schedule (Algorithm 2)
7  $q_{ui} \leftarrow [z_{u}; z_{i}] \pi_{ui} \leftarrow \text{Softmax}(\text{Router}(q_{ui}; \Theta_{rt}))$  // dense routing (pre Top-K)
8 Step 2: Sparse expert activation &amp; modality-guided aggregation
9  $\Gamma_{ui} \leftarrow \text{TopK}(\pi_{ui}, K)$  // activate K experts
10  $\tilde{\pi}_{ui} \leftarrow \text{Renorm}(\pi_{ui}, \Gamma_{ui})$  //  $\sum_{e \in \Gamma_{ui}} \tilde{\pi}_{ui}^{(e)} = 1$ 
11  $s_{ui} \leftarrow 0$ 
12 foreach  $e \in \Gamma_{ui}$  do // template-guided expert forward
13
14  $s_{ui}^{(e)} \leftarrow \text{Expert}_{e}(z_{u}, z_{i}, \{h_{u}^{m}\}_{m\in M}, \{h_{i}^{m}\}_{m\in M}; \Theta_{e})$ 
15  $s_{ui} \leftarrow s_{ui} + \tilde{\pi}_{ui}^{(e)} \cdot s_{ui}^{(e)}$ 
16 end
17 Step 3: Scoring
18  $\hat{y}_{ui} \leftarrow \text{Score}(s_{ui})$ 
19 return ( $\hat{y}_{ui}, \pi_{ui}, \Gamma_{ui}, \tilde{\pi}_{ui}$ )
</div>

For clarit<sub>y</sub>, we summarize the full MoE-based scorin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>rocedure (from routin<sub>g</sub> to a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation and <sub>p</sub>rediction) in Al<sub>g</sub>orithm 1, which will be reused in the trainin<sub>g p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline.

3.3.1 Modality cues: item projection and history-induced user cues. Let M denote the item content modalit<sub>y</sub> set (default $M = \{ A , S \} )$ . For eac<sup>h</sup> item �, we compute projecte<sup>d</sup> mo<sup>d</sup>a<sup>l</sup>ity cues

$$
h _ {i} ^ {A} = f _ {A} (x _ {i} ^ {A}), \qquad h _ {i} ^ {S} = f _ {S} (x _ {i} ^ {S}),\tag{7}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $x _ { i } ^ { m }$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e raw mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>lit<sub>y-�</sub> f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ure an</sub>d $f _ { m } ( \cdot )$ is a <sup>l</sup>ig<sup>h</sup>tweig<sup>h</sup>t <sup>l</sup>inear projection. Since user-si<sup>d</sup>e <sub>raw</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> <sub>pro</sub>fil<sub>es</sub> <sub>are</sub> t<sub>yp</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>unava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub> i<sub>mp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>,</sub> <sub>we</sub> d<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ve</sub> <sub>wea</sub>k <sub>user</sub> <sub>cues</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> training-only histories:

$$
h _ {u} ^ {A} = \frac {1}{| I _ {u} |} \sum_ {i \in I _ {u}} h _ {i} ^ {A}, \qquad h _ {u} ^ {S} = \frac {1}{| I _ {u} |} \sum_ {i \in I _ {u}} h _ {i} ^ {S},\tag{8}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $I _ { u }$ is the trainin<sub>g</sub>-onl<sub>y</sub> interacted item set of user � (Table 1), constructed solel<sub>y</sub> from observed trainin<sub>g</sub> interactions. This desi<sub>g</sub>n kee<sub>p</sub>s behavior embeddin<sub>g</sub>s $( z _ { u } , z _ { i } )$ <sub>as</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>t <sub>user</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>gna</sub>l<sub>,</sub> while allowin<sub>g</sub> contents to <sub>p</sub>artici<sub>p</sub>ate throu<sub>g</sub>h histor<sub>y</sub>-induced cues without leakin<sub>g</sub> test-time inf<sub>o</sub>rm<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>.</sub>

3.3.2 Triplet experts with shared templates. We reuse the shorthand Behavior/Appearance/Semantics as B/A/S (Section 3.1.1). Define modalit<sub>y</sub>-s<sub>p</sub>ecific re<sub>p</sub>resentations under the <sub>g</sub>lobal order [�, �, �]:

$$
\mathbf {r} _ {u} ^ {B} = \mathbf {z} _ {u}, \quad \mathbf {r} _ {i} ^ {B} = \mathbf {z} _ {i}, \qquad \mathbf {r} _ {u} ^ {A} = \mathbf {h} _ {u} ^ {A}, \mathbf {r} _ {i} ^ {A} = \mathbf {h} _ {i} ^ {A}, \qquad \mathbf {r} _ {u} ^ {S} = \mathbf {h} _ {u} ^ {S}, \mathbf {r} _ {i} ^ {S} = \mathbf {h} _ {i} ^ {S}.\tag{9}
$$

Each ex<sub>p</sub>ert is s<sub>p</sub>ecified b<sub>y</sub> a nonne<sub>g</sub>ative modalit<sub>y</sub> tri<sub>p</sub>let $\textbf { w } = ~ [ w ^ { B } , w ^ { A } , w ^ { S } ] ^ { \top } ~ \in ~ \mathbb { R } _ { \ge 0 } ^ { 3 }$ <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> <sub>norma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d b<sub>u</sub>d<sub>ge</sub>t $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { m \in \{ B , A , S \} } w ^ { m } = 1 } \end{array}$ . Given an expert weight triplet w, we form expert-specific fused user/item re<sub>p</sub>resentations b<sub>y</sub> a sim<sub>p</sub>le<sub>,</sub> inter<sub>p</sub>retable tri<sub>p</sub>let fusion:

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {u} (\mathbf {w}) = \sum_ {m \in \{B, A, S \}} w ^ {m} \mathbf {r} _ {u} ^ {m}, \qquad \mathbf {x} _ {i} (\mathbf {w}) = \sum_ {m \in \{B, A, S \}} w ^ {m} \mathbf {r} _ {i} ^ {m}.\tag{10}
$$

An ex<sub>p</sub>ert module then <sub>p</sub>roduces a <sub>p</sub>air re<sub>p</sub>resentation (the internal head can be li<sub>g</sub>htwei<sub>g</sub>ht and ex<sub>p</sub>ert-s<sub>p</sub>ecific):

$$
\mathbf {s} _ {u i} ^ {(e)} = \mathrm{Expert} _ {e} \big (\mathbf {z} _ {u}, \mathbf {z} _ {i}, \{\mathbf {h} _ {u} ^ {m} \} _ {m \in \mathcal {M}}, \{\mathbf {h} _ {i} ^ {m} \} _ {m \in \mathcal {M}}; \Theta_ {e} \big) = \phi \Big ([ \mathbf {x} _ {u} (\mathbf {w} ^ {(e)}); \mathbf {x} _ {i} (\mathbf {w} ^ {(e)}) ]; \Theta_ {e} \Big),\tag{11}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\mathbf { w } ^ { ( e ) }$ is the tri<sub>p</sub>let of ex<sub>p</sub>ert � (defined below), and $\phi (  { \boldsymbol { \cdot } } , \Theta _ { e } )$ is a li<sub>g</sub>htwei<sub>g</sub>ht transformation. Thi<sub>s</sub> t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>p</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t f<sub>orm</sub> <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>es</sub> <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>exper</sub>t di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpre</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>ena</sub>bl<sub>es</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ean</sub> <sub>a</sub>bl<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>over</sub> f<sub>us</sub>i<sub>on</sub> p<sup>atterns.</sup>

Core definitions: expert groups, expert types, and template instantiation. To make the expert pool ex<sub>p</sub>licit and inter<sub>p</sub>retable, we formalize ex<sub>p</sub>ert <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s (b<sub>y</sub> anchor modalit<sub>y</sub>) and shared t<sub>yp</sub>e-level tem<sub>p</sub><sup>l</sup>ates.

(1) Expert groups (anchor modality). We organize experts into three modality groups $g \in$ {�, �, �}, where each <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> s<sub>p</sub>ecifies an anchor source(followin<sub>g</sub> Section 3.1.1). The anchor is a semantic label for structuring and analyzing experts; it does not hard-assign instances. This design enables <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>-wise anal<sub>y</sub>sis (e.<sub>g</sub>., utilization b<sub>y</sub> anchor) and <sub>y</sub>ields a com<sub>p</sub>act $3 \times 3$ <sup>ex</sup>p<sup>ert</sup> p<sup>ool</sup> without introducin<sub>g</sub> additional <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>-s<sub>p</sub>ecific h<sub>yp</sub>er<sub>p</sub>arameters.

(2) Expert types (how the anchor participates). Within each group, we instantiate three expert types � ∈ {Dom, Bal, Com}: (i) Dominant (Dom): the anchor receives a large portion of the fusion bud<sub>g</sub>et, with auxiliaries down-wei<sub>g</sub>hted (and <sub>p</sub>ossibl<sub>y</sub> a<sub>pp</sub>roachin<sub>g</sub> zero at the boundar<sub>y</sub>); (ii) Balanced (Bal): a smoother trade-of with a bounded anchor bias; (iii) Complementary (Com): auxiliaries provide the major evidence while retaining a small anchor mass.

(3) Type-level templates in canonical order. A type-level template maps a scalar to a canonical tri<sub>p</sub>let $\tilde { \mathbf { w } } ^ { t } ( \cdot ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ under the order [anchor, aux<sub>1</sub>, aux<sub>2</sub>]:

$$
\tilde {\mathbf {w}} ^ {\mathrm{Dom}} (\alpha) = (1 - \alpha) \left[ \frac {1}{2}, \frac {1}{4}, \frac {1}{4} \right] + \alpha [ 1, 0, 0 ], \quad \alpha \in [ 0, 1 ],\tag{12}
$$

$$
\tilde {\mathbf {w}} ^ {\mathrm{Bal}} (\beta) = \left[ \frac {1}{3} + \frac {\beta}{6}, \frac {1}{3} - \frac {\beta}{1 2}, \frac {1}{3} - \frac {\beta}{1 2} \right], \qquad \beta \in [ 0, 1 ],\tag{13}
$$

$$
\tilde {\mathbf {w}} ^ {\mathrm{Com}} (\delta) = \left[ \epsilon , (1 - \epsilon) \delta , (1 - \epsilon) (1 - \delta) \right], \qquad \delta \in [ 0, 1 ],\tag{14}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\delta$ <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e sp</sub>lit b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo aux</sub>ili<sub>ary mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>liti<sub>es an</sub>d $\epsilon > 0$ i<sub>s a sma</sub>ll fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>an</sub>t encoding a minimal anchor mass. When $g = B$ <sub>,</sub> this corres<sub>p</sub>onds to retainin<sub>g</sub> a minimal behavior <sub>anc</sub>h<sub>or, re</sub>fl<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> bi<sub>as o</sub>f i<sub>mp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>; w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> $g \in \{ A , S \}$ <sub>,</sub> it <sub>preven</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e anc</sub>h<sub>or</sub> <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>lit<sub>y</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> b<sub>e</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>en</sub>ti<sub>re</sub>l<sub>y</sub> di<sub>scar</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d<sub>.</sub> W<sub>e</sub> t<sub>rea</sub>t $\alpha , \beta , \delta$ as template hyperparameters: they are t<sub>yp</sub>e-level shared and fixed durin<sub>g</sub> trainin<sub>g,</sub> and we anal<sub>y</sub>ze their sensitivit<sub>y</sub> in Section 4.7.

(4) From templates to a $3 \times 3$ expert pool (group-specific instantiation). To obtain the final ex<sub>p</sub>ert tri<sub>p</sub>let in the <sub>g</sub>lobal order [�, �, �], we a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> a fixed <sub>p</sub>ermutation o<sub>p</sub>erator $P _ { g } ( \cdot )$ t<sup>h</sup>at ma<sub>p</sub>s the canonical order [anchor, aux<sub>1</sub>, aux<sub>2</sub>] to [�, �, �] accordin<sub>g</sub> to <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> <sub>�</sub>:

$$
\mathbf {w} ^ {g, t} = P _ {g} \big (\tilde {\mathbf {w}} ^ {t} \big), \qquad g \in \{B, A, S \}, t \in \{\text { Dom }, \text { Bal }, \text { Com } \}.\tag{15}
$$

$P _ { g } ( \cdot )$ i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>erm</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>c an</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>d<sub>uces no</sub> l<sub>earna</sub>bl<sub>e parame</sub>t<sub>ers;</sub> th<sub>us</sub> th<sub>e exper</sub>t <sub>poo</sub>l i<sub>s</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y spec</sub>ifi<sub>e</sub>d once $( g , t )$ are chosen. We index ex<sub>p</sub>erts b<sub>y</sub> $e  ( g , t )$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>y</sub>ieldin<sub>g</sub> $E = 9$ <sub>exper</sub>t<sub>s</sub> b<sub>y</sub> d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>au</sub>lt<sub>.</sub>

3.3.3 Behavior-conditioned routing with dense distributions. Given a training instance $( u , i )$ <sub>,</sub> MAG-NET <sub>rou</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>correspon</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>sma</sub>ll <sub>su</sub>b<sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>exper</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ure</sub>d <sub>p</sub>ool in Section 3.3.2. Because user-side raw modalit<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>rofiles are not directl<sub>y</sub> observed in im<sub>p</sub>licit f<sub>ee</sub>db<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>, we con</sub>diti<sub>on rou</sub>ti<sub>ng on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>use</sub>d b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>or em</sub>b<sub>e</sub>ddi<sub>ngs</sub> f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>ua</sub>l<sub>-v</sub>i<sub>ew</sub> b<sub>ac</sub>kb<sub>one</sub> b<sub>y</sub> <sup>f</sup>orm<sup>i</sup>n<sub>g</sub> t<sup>h</sup>e router <sub>q</sub>uer<sub>y</sub>

$$
q _ {u i} = [ z _ {u}; z _ {i} ] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {2 d}.\tag{16}
$$

Although the router is behavior-conditioned, the expert space is modality-guided: each expert im<sub>p</sub>lements a distinct tri<sub>p</sub>let fusion over $\{ B , A , S \}$ <sub>an</sub>d <sub>consumes mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>lit<sub>y cues</sub> $\{ h _ { u } ^ { m } , h _ { i } ^ { m } \} _ { m \in \mathcal { M } }$ throu<sub>g</sub>h E<sub>q</sub>s. (7) to (10).

Our <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressive routin<sub>g</sub> scheme follows standard s<sub>p</sub>arse MoE trainin<sub>g,</sub> while addin<sub>g</sub> an entro<sub>py</sub>- triggered regularization schedule to make routing both usable (avoid early expert collapse/load imbalance) and adaptive (reduce manual guidance as training proceeds). The key idea is: we <sub>p</sub>re-structure ex<sub>p</sub>erts into modalit<sub>y</sub>-ali<sub>g</sub>ned families via fixed tem<sub>p</sub>lates (section 3.3), then use routing-entropy statistics to progressively relax regularization, transitioning from template-guided ex<sub>p</sub>loration to more data-driven <sub>g</sub>atin<sub>g</sub>.

S<sub>pec</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> $, q _ { u i }$ is defined in (16) and produces a dense routing distribution over all � experts:

$$
\boldsymbol {\pi} _ {u i} = \operatorname{Softmax} (\operatorname{Router} (\mathbf {q} _ {u i}; \Theta_ {\mathrm{rt}})) \in \mathbb {R} ^ {E}.\tag{17}
$$

We kee<sub>p</sub> $\pmb { \pi } _ { u i }$ as dense <sub>p</sub>robabilities for (i) com<sub>p</sub>utin<sub>g</sub> entro<sub>py</sub>-based statistics and (ii) a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> sta<sub>g</sub>e-wise routin<sub>g</sub> re<sub>g</sub>ularization<sub>,</sub> while the forward a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation still uses s<sub>p</sub>arse To<sub>p</sub>-� ex<sub>p</sub>erts (described in Section 3.3.4). Over a mini-batch B, we a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ate $\{ \pi _ { u i } \} _ { ( u , i ) \in \mathcal { B } }$ t<sub>o es</sub>tim<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> r<sub>ou</sub>tin<sub>g</sub> entro<sub>py</sub> and tri<sub>gg</sub>er sta<sub>g</sub>e switchin<sub>g</sub> in Section 3.4.

3.3.4 Sparse expert activation, aggregation, and scoring. For eficient computation, we activate only the To<sub>p</sub>-� ex<sub>p</sub>erts <sub>u</sub>nder the dense distrib<sub>u</sub>tion:

$$
\Gamma_ {u i} = \mathrm{TopK} (\pi_ {u i}, K).\tag{18}
$$

Followin<sub>g</sub> standard s<sub>p</sub>arse MoE<sub>,</sub> we renormalize routin<sub>g</sub> wei<sub>g</sub>hts on $\Gamma _ { u i }$ <sub>an</sub>d <sub>use</sub> th<sub>e renorma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d we<sup>i</sup><sub>g</sub><sup>h</sup>ts <sup>f</sup>or a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>at<sup>i</sup>on:

$$
\tilde {\pi} _ {u i} ^ {(e)} = \frac {\pi_ {u i} ^ {(e)}}{\sum_ {e ^ {\prime} \in \Gamma_ {u i}} \pi_ {u i} ^ {(e ^ {\prime})}}, \qquad e \in \Gamma_ {u i}.\tag{19}
$$

Each activated ex<sub>p</sub>ert $e \in  { \Gamma } _ { u i }$ <sub>p</sub>roduces an ex<sub>p</sub>ert-s<sub>p</sub>ecific <sub>p</sub>air re<sub>p</sub>resentation $\mathbf { s } _ { u i } ^ { ( e ) }$ (E<sub>q</sub>. (11)), and we <sup>a</sup>gg<sup>re</sup>g<sup>ate</sup> <sup>them</sup> <sup>b</sup>y <sup>routin</sup>g <sup>wei</sup>g<sup>hts:</sup>

$$
s _ {u i} = \sum_ {e \in \Gamma_ {u i}} \tilde {\pi} _ {u i} ^ {(e)} s _ {u i} ^ {(e)}.
$$

(20)

Finall<sub>y,</sub> we obtain the <sub>p</sub>reference <sub>p</sub>rediction b<sub>y</sub> a scorin<sub>g</sub> head:

$$
\hat {y} _ {u i} = \mathrm{Score} (s _ {u i}).\tag{21}
$$

In all ex<sub>p</sub>eriments we use $\tilde { \pi } _ { u i }$ for a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation<sub>;</sub> the dense $\pi _ { u i }$ is retained for entro<sub>py</sub> statistics and <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressive routin<sub>g</sub> re<sub>g</sub>ularization (3.4). We ado<sub>p</sub>t standard s<sub>p</sub>arse MoE trainin<sub>g</sub>: <sub>g</sub>radients are b<sub>ac</sub>k<sub>-propaga</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d th<sub>roug</sub>h th<sub>e se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>exper</sub>t<sub>s an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r correspon</sub>di<sub>ng rou</sub>t<sub>er we</sub>i<sub>g</sub>ht<sub>s.</sub>

<div class="mineru-algorithm" style="white-space: pre-wrap; font-family:monospace;">
Algorithm 2: Progressive Routing Training of MAGNET

Require: Observed edges E; graphs  $G_{UI}$  and  $G_{UIG}$  (if  $E^{+} \neq \emptyset$ ); mini-batch  $B \subset E$  with  $b := |B|$ , neg. ratio  $\rho$ ; experts  $(E, K)$ ; entropy  $(H^{*}, W)$ ; weights  $(\lambda, \lambda_{\mathrm{ctr}}, \lambda_{r})$ ; temperature  $\tau$ ; iterations T; params  $\Theta$ 

Ensure: Trained parameters  $\Theta$ 

2 stage ← 1;  $n \leftarrow 0$    // stage ∈ {1, 2}, counter for consecutive  $\tilde{H} \geq H^{*}$ 

3 for t ← 1 to T do

4 Sample  $B \subset E$  with  $|B| = b$    // (I) mini-batch sampling on observed edges

5 foreach  $(u, i) \in B$  do

6 Sample negatives  $J_{u} \sim \text{NEG}(u; \rho)$  with  $j \notin I_{u}$ 

7  $L_{BPR} \leftarrow 0; \Pi \leftarrow \emptyset$    // (II) BPR loss + collect dense routings

8 foreach  $(u, i) \in B$  do

9  $(\hat{y}_{ui}, \pi_{ui}) \leftarrow \text{MAGNET-SCORE}(u, i; \Theta) \Pi \leftarrow \Pi \cup \{\pi_{ui}\} \{\hat{y}_{uj}\}_{j \in J_{u}} \leftarrow \text{MAGNET-SCORE}(u, J_{u}; \Theta).\hat{y}$    // reuse Alg. 1 (score only)

10  $L_{BPR} += \sum_{j \in J_{u}} -\log \sigma(\hat{y}_{ui} - \hat{y}_{uj})$ 

11  $L_{ctr} \leftarrow 0$    // (III) optional dual-view alignment

12 if  $E^{+} \neq \emptyset$  and  $\lambda_{ctr} &gt; 0$  then    // dual-view only

13  $L_{ctr} \leftarrow INFONCE(\cdot; \tau)$    // align  $z^{UI}$  vs  $z^{UIG}$  Section 3.4.3

14  $\bar{\pi} \leftarrow \frac{1}{|\Pi|} \sum_{\pi \in \Pi} \pi; \tilde{H} \leftarrow Ent(\bar{\pi}) / log E$    // (IV) Eqs. (23) and (24)

15 if  $\tilde{H} \geq H^{*}$  then

16  $n \leftarrow n + 1$    // consecutive “high-entropy” steps

17 else

18  $n \leftarrow 0$ 

19 if stage = 1 and n ≥ W then    // switch once

20 stage ← 2    // enter sharpening stage

21  $L_{cov} \leftarrow \sum_{e=1}^{E} \left( \bar{\pi}(e) - \frac{1}{E} \right)^{2}$    // load balancing, Equation (29)

22  $L_{conf} \leftarrow \frac{1}{|\Pi|} \sum_{\pi \in \Pi} Ent(\pi)$    // routing sharpness, Equation (30)

23  $(\lambda_{\mathrm{cov}}, \lambda_{\mathrm{conf}}) \leftarrow \lambda_{r} \cdot (\mathbb{I}[stage = 1](1 - \tilde{H}), \mathbb{I}[stage = 2]\tilde{H})$ 

24  $L_{\ell} &lt; L_{BPR} + \lambda_{ctr} L_{ctr} + \lambda_{cov} L_{cov} + \lambda_{conf} L_{conf} + \lambda \|O\|_{2}^{2}$
</div>

## 3.4 Entropy-triggered Progressive Routing for Training

3.4.1 Routing Entropy and Efective Experts. We summarize the full training procedure with entropytri<sub>gg</sub>ered <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressive routin<sub>g</sub> in Al<sub>g</sub>orithm 2<sub>,</sub> and describe its ke<sub>y</sub> com<sub>p</sub>onents below.

Given a mini-batch B, we define the batch-mean routin<sub>g</sub> distribution

$$
\bar {\pi} = \frac {1}{| \mathcal {B} |} \sum_ {(u, i) \in \mathcal {B}} \pi_ {u i},\tag{22}
$$

and com<sub>p</sub>ute its Shannon entro<sub>py</sub> (usin<sub>g</sub> natural lo<sub>g</sub>arithm)

$$
H (\bar {\pi}) = - \sum_ {e = 1} ^ {E} \bar {\pi} (e) \log \bar {\pi} (e),\tag{23}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $H ( \bar { \pmb { \pi } } ) \in [ 0 , \log E ]$

Interpretation. Note that $H ( { \bar { \pmb { \pi } } } )$ measures batch-level utilization diversity: it becomes large when diferent trainin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>airs in the batch are routed to diferent ex<sub>p</sub>erts in a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ate. This is deliberatel<sub>y</sub> <sub>use</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>th<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e rou</sub>t<sub>er</sub> h<sub>as escape</sub>d <sub>ear</sub>l<sub>y co</sub>ll<sub>apse an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ar</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o u</sub>tili<sub>ze a</sub> b<sub>roa</sub>d <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f ex<sub>p</sub>erts<sub>,</sub> even when <sub>p</sub>er-instance routin<sub>g</sub> can alread<sub>y</sub> be shar<sub>p</sub>.

For com<sub>p</sub>arabilit<sub>y</sub> across diferent �<sub>,</sub> we also use the normalized entro<sub>py</sub>

$$
\tilde {H} = \frac {H (\bar {\pi})}{\log E} \in [ 0, 1 ],\tag{24}
$$

which will be used to control the stren<sub>g</sub>th of routin<sub>g</sub> re<sub>g</sub>ularization in Section 3.4.3. Finall<sub>y,</sub> we <sub>repor</sub>t th<sub>e e</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ve num</sub>b<sub>er o</sub>f <sub>exper</sub>t<sub>s</sub>

$$
N _ {\mathrm{eff}} = \exp \bigl (H (\bar {\pi}) \bigr),\tag{25}
$$

<sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h i<sub>s</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>tl<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpre</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>as</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>num</sub>b<sub>er</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>un</sub>if<sub>orm</sub>l<sub>y-use</sub>d <sub>exper</sub>t<sub>s.</sub>

3.4.2 Entropy-triggered Two-stage Schedule. We adopt an entropy-triggered two-stage schedule to progressively transition from guided exploration to adaptive exploitation in MoE routing. Intuitively, <sub>ear</sub>l<sub>y</sub> i<sub>n</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>rou</sub>t<sub>er</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>uns</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>may</sub> <sub>co</sub>ll<sub>apse</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> f<sub>ew</sub> <sub>exper</sub>t<sub>s;</sub> th<sub>us</sub> <sub>we</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>encourage</sub> broad ex<sub>p</sub>ert utilization. Once the router reaches suficientl<sub>y</sub> diverse utilization<sub>,</sub> we switch to a second sta<sub>g</sub>e that shar<sub>p</sub>ens and stabilizes routin<sub>g</sub> for more consistent ex<sub>p</sub>ert s<sub>p</sub>ecialization.

Concretel<sub>y,</sub> at each trainin<sub>g</sub> ste<sub>p</sub> we com<sub>p</sub>ute the normalized batch entro<sub>py</sub> $\tilde { H }$ in E<sub>q</sub>. (24) and u<sub>p</sub><sup>d</sup>ate a counter �:

$$
n \leftarrow \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} n + 1, & \text { if } \tilde {H} \geq H ^ {*}, \\ 0, & \text { otherwise }, \end{array} \right. \quad s t a g e \leftarrow \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} 2, & \text { if } n \geq W, \\ s t a g e, & \text { otherwise }, \end{array} \right.\tag{26}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $H ^ { * } \in [ 0 , 1 ]$ is an entro<sub>py</sub> threshold and � is a window size for re<sub>q</sub>uirin<sub>g</sub> the condition to h<sub>o</sub>ld <sub>cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>tl<sub>y.</sub>

Stage semantics. Stage 1 (coverage) emphasizes utilization to avoid early collapse; Stage 2 (confidence) em<sub>p</sub>hasizes shar<sub>p</sub>er and more stable routin<sub>g</sub> after utilization becomes suficientl<sub>y</sub> diverse. Th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>age-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> <sub>regu</sub>l<sub>ar</sub>i<sub>zers</sub> <sub>are</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> <sub>nex</sub>t <sub>par</sub>t<sub>.</sub>

3.4.3 Objective with Stage-specific Routing Regularization. For preference learning, we use a pair-<sub>w</sub>i<sub>se</sub> BPR l<sub>oss</sub> <sub>o</sub>n tri<sub>p</sub>l<sub>e</sub>t<sub>s</sub> $( u , i , j )$ <sub>w</sub>ith $( u , i ) \in \mathcal { D } _ { t r }$ <sub>� an</sub>d $j \notin \mathcal { I } _ { u }$ :

$$
\mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{BPR}} = \sum_ {(u, i, j)} - \log \sigma (\hat {y} _ {u i} - \hat {y} _ {u j}).\tag{27}
$$

O<sub>p</sub>tionall<sub>y,</sub> for the dual-view settin<sub>g,</sub> we add a view-contrastive ali<sub>g</sub>nment loss $L _ { \mathrm { c t r } }$ t<sub>o re</sub>d<sub>uce</sub> the re<sub>p</sub>resentation discre<sub>p</sub>anc<sub>y</sub> between the two structural views. Given a mini-batch $B \subset { \mathcal { E } }$ <sub>,</sub> l<sub>e</sub>t $\mathcal { U } _ { B } : = \{ u \ | \ ( u , i ) \in B \}$ <sub>an</sub>d ${ \mathcal { I } } _ { B } : = \{ i \ | \ ( u , i ) \in B \}$ denote the user/item sets a<sub>pp</sub>earin<sub>g</sub> in �. We treat $( z _ { u } ^ { U I } , z _ { u } ^ { U I G } )$ <sub>an</sub>d $( z _ { i } ^ { U I } , z _ { i } ^ { U I G } )$ as <sub>p</sub>ositive <sub>p</sub>airs<sub>,</sub> and use other users/items within the same mini-batch as ne<sub>g</sub>atives. We use a s<sub>y</sub>mmetric (bi-directional) InfoNCE:

$$
L _ {\mathrm{ctr}} = \frac {1}{2} \left(L _ {\mathrm{ctr}} ^ {U I \rightarrow U I G} + L _ {\mathrm{ctr}} ^ {U I G \rightarrow U I}\right),\tag{28a}
$$

$$
L _ {\mathrm{ctr}, U} ^ {a \rightarrow b} = \frac {1}{| U _ {B} |} \sum_ {u \in U _ {B}} \left[ - \log \frac {\exp (\mathrm{sim} (z _ {u} ^ {a} , z _ {u} ^ {b}) / \tau)}{\sum_ {u ^ {\prime} \in U _ {B}} \exp (\mathrm{sim} (z _ {u} ^ {a} , z _ {u ^ {\prime}} ^ {b}) / \tau)} \right],\tag{28b}
$$

$$
L _ {\mathrm{ctr}, I} ^ {a \rightarrow b} = \frac {1}{| I _ {B} |} \sum_ {i \in I _ {B}} \left[ - \log \frac {\exp (\mathrm{sim} (z _ {i} ^ {a} , z _ {i} ^ {b}) / \tau)}{\sum_ {i ^ {\prime} \in I _ {B}} \exp (\mathrm{sim} (z _ {i} ^ {a} , z _ {i ^ {\prime}} ^ {b}) / \tau)} \right],\tag{28c}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $a , b \in \{ U I , U I G \} , L _ { \mathrm { c t r } } ^ { a  b } = L _ { \mathrm { c t r } , U } ^ { a  b } + L _ { \mathrm { c t r } , I } ^ { a  b }$ <sub>, an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>i<sub>m</sub> $\begin{array} { r } { ( x , y ) = \frac { x ^ { \top } y } { \| x \| _ { 2 } \| y \| _ { 2 } } } \end{array}$

Stage-specific routing regularizers. We regularize the dense routing distribution $\pmb { \pi } _ { u i }$ <sup>i</sup>n a sta<sub>g</sub>es<sub>p</sub>ecific manner: sta<sub>g</sub>e 1 encoura<sub>g</sub>es utilization (covera<sub>g</sub>e) while sta<sub>g</sub>e 2 encoura<sub>g</sub>es shar<sub>p</sub>er and more stable routin<sub>g</sub> (confidence).

Coverage regularizer (Stage 1). To encourage broad expert utilization and avoid early expert collapse, we regularize the mini-batch mean routing distribution �¯ toward uniform:

$$
\mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{cov}} = \sum_ {e = 1} ^ {E} \left(\bar {\pi} (e) - \frac {1}{E}\right) ^ {2}.\tag{29}
$$

<sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>pena</sub>li<sub>zes</sub> l<sub>oa</sub>d i<sub>m</sub>b<sub>a</sub>l<sub>ance a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h l<sub>eve</sub>l <sub>an</sub>d i<sub>s</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e w.r.</sub>t<sub>. rou</sub>t<sub>er parame-</sub> ters.

Confidence regularizer (Stage 2). To stabilize specialization after utilization becomes diverse, we encoura<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>er-<sup>i</sup>nstance rout<sup>i</sup>n<sub>g</sub> to <sup>b</sup>e s<sup>h</sup>ar<sub>p</sub>:

$$
\mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{conf}} = \frac {1}{| \mathcal {B} |} \sum_ {(u, i) \in \mathcal {B}} H (\boldsymbol {\pi} _ {u i}),\tag{30}
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $H ( \pmb { \pi } _ { u i } )$ is defined analo<sub>g</sub>ousl<sub>y</sub> to E<sub>q</sub>. (23).

Entropy-controlled weights. To realize progressive relaxation, we gate the stage and also modulate the re<sub>g</sub>ularization stren<sub>g</sub>th b<sub>y</sub> the normalized entro<sub>py</sub> $\tilde { H } { : }$

$$
\lambda_ {\mathrm{cov}} (H) = \mathbb {I} [ s t a g e = 1 ] \cdot \lambda_ {r} \cdot (1 - \tilde {H}), \qquad \lambda_ {\mathrm{conf}} (H) = \mathbb {I} [ s t a g e = 2 ] \cdot \lambda_ {r} \cdot \tilde {H}.\tag{31}
$$

This kee<sub>p</sub>s a sin<sub>g</sub>le tunable routin<sub>g</sub> wei<sub>g</sub>ht $\lambda _ { r }$ <sub>,</sub> while makin<sub>g</sub> the efective re<sub>g</sub>ularization automaticall<sub>y</sub> decrease as routin<sub>g</sub> becomes more diverse (Sta<sub>g</sub>e 1) and self-adjust as routin<sub>g</sub> shar<sub>p</sub>ens (Sta<sub>g</sub>e 2).

Putting everyt<sup>h</sup>ing toget<sup>h</sup>er, t<sup>h</sup>e overa<sup>ll</sup> o<sup>b</sup>jective is

$$
\mathcal {L} = \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{BPR}} + \lambda_ {\mathrm{ctr}} \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{ctr}} + \lambda_ {\mathrm{cov}} (H) \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{cov}} + \lambda_ {\mathrm{conf}} (H) \mathcal {L} _ {\mathrm{conf}} + \lambda \| \Theta \| _ {2} ^ {2},\tag{32}
$$

where Θ collects all trainable <sub>p</sub>arameters (encoder/<sub>p</sub>rojection/router/ex<sub>p</sub>erts).

## 4.3 Training and Evaluation Protocol

4.3.1 Training Setup and Reproducibility. All methods are trained and evaluated under the same <sub>p</sub>re<sub>p</sub>rocessin<sub>g p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline and the fixed data s<sub>p</sub>lits described in Section 4.1 to ensure a fair com<sub>p</sub>arison. F<sub>or</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ma</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>compar</sub>i<sub>son</sub> <sub>resu</sub>lt<sub>s,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>repor</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mean</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>s</sub>t<sub>an</sub>d<sub>ar</sub>d d<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>over</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>ve</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d s<sub>p</sub>lits<sub>,</sub> while anal<sub>y</sub>sis ex<sub>p</sub>eriments such as ablations and sensitivit<sub>y</sub> studies are conducted on the fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>sp</sub>lit <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>see</sub>d 2026<sub>.</sub>

We o<sub>p</sub>timize models with mini-batch trainin<sub>g</sub>. Unless otherwise s<sub>p</sub>ecified<sub>,</sub> the batch size is 1024 and we use uniform ne<sub>g</sub>ative sam<sub>p</sub>lin<sub>g</sub> with a 1:1 <sub>p</sub>ositive-to-ne<sub>g</sub>ative ratio (each <sub>p</sub>ositive interaction is <sub>p</sub>aired with one unobserved item). We use Adam for o<sub>p</sub>timization and a<sub>pp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> earl<sub>y</sub> sto<sub>pp</sub>in<sub>g</sub> on the validation set with a <sub>p</sub>atience of 5 and a maximum of 200 e<sub>p</sub>ochs. For each run<sub>,</sub> the check<sub>p</sub>oint is selected b<sub>y</sub> the best validation <sub>p</sub>erformance (b<sub>y</sub> default, NDCG@20).

CPU and 120 GB RAM. Table 3 reports the hyperparameter settings used i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e ma</sub>i<sub>n exper</sub>i<sub>men</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> F<sub>or eac</sub>h d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>, we se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>a s</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>e con</sub>fi<sub>gura</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>on va</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>per</sub>f<sub>ormance</sub> <sub>an</sub>d k<sub>eep</sub> it fi<sub>xe</sub>d f<sub>or</sub> <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>su</sub>b<sub>sequen</sub>t <sub>runs</sub> <sub>on</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>.</sub> M<sub>o</sub>d<sub>e</sub>l <sub>se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ows</sub> S<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> 4<sub>.</sub>3<sub>:</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>mon</sub>it<sub>or</sub> <sub>va</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> NDCG<sub>@</sub>20 f<sub>or</sub> <sub>ear</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>opp</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>use</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>h<sub>ec</sub>k<sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub> best validation score for re<sub>p</sub>ortin<sub>g</sub>. This setu<sub>p</sub> kee<sub>p</sub>s the com<sub>p</sub>arison consistent and reduces tunin<sub>g</sub> decisions that de<sub>p</sub>end on a <sub>p</sub>articular s<sub>p</sub>lit.

4.4.2 Stage-wise Tuning Procedure. Since MAGNET involves multiple interacting components, tuning a<sup>ll</sup> <sup>h</sup>yperparameters joint<sup>l</sup>y is computationa<sup>ll</sup>y expensive an<sup>d</sup> <sup>d</sup>i<sup>fi</sup>cu<sup>l</sup>t to interpret. We t<sup>h</sup>ere<sup>f</sup>ore a<sup>d</sup>o<sub>p</sub>t a sta<sub>g</sub>e-w<sup>i</sup>se <sub>p</sub>roce<sup>d</sup>ure: we tune one <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> o<sup>f</sup> <sup>h</sup><sub>yp</sub>er<sub>p</sub>arameters at a t<sup>i</sup>me, carr<sub>y</sub> t<sup>h</sup>e b<sub>es</sub>t <sub>se</sub>tti<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>orwar</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e nex</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>age, an</sub>d <sub>se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>pr</sub>i<sub>mar</sub>il<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y va</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> NDCG<sub>@</sub>20<sub>.</sub>

## 4.5 Overall Performance

Table 4 reports the top-N recommendation results on four multimodal datasets. We use MAGNET -DV as our default settin<sub>g</sub> and com<sub>p</sub>are it a<sub>g</sub>ainst other baselines. Overall<sub>,</sub> MAGNET delivers the stron<sub>g</sub>est and most stable <sub>p</sub>erformance across datasets and metrics<sub>,</sub> indicatin<sub>g</sub> that to<sub>p</sub>olo<sub>gy</sub>-aware <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>ation<sub>,</sub> our tri<sub>p</sub>let-tem<sub>p</sub>late MoE fusion<sub>,</sub> and <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressive routin<sub>g</sub> are com<sub>p</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> for <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>l <sub>recommen</sub>d<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

– Consistent gains over strong SOTA baselines. Across the four datasets, MAGNET (Dualview) achieves an average relative improvement of about 3.0%–5.3% over the strongest non-MAGNET baseline (Bab<sub>y</sub>: ∼3.0%, S<sub>p</sub>orts: ∼4.5%, Clothin<sub>g</sub>: ∼4.1%, Electronics: ∼5.3%). This <sub>p</sub>attern is consistent in both Recall and NDCG<sub>,</sub> su<sub>gg</sub>estin<sub>g</sub> that the im<sub>p</sub>rovement is not metric-s<sub>p</sub>ecific. H<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>e</sub> “<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ronges</sub>t b<sub>ase</sub>li<sub>ne</sub>” i<sub>s se</sub>l<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>per</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t b<sub>y va</sub>lid<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> NDCG<sub>@</sub>20<sub>, an</sub>d <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>num</sub>b<sub>ers</sub> <sub>are avera e</sub>d <sub>over</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>ve</sub> fi<sub>xe</sub>d <sub>s</sub> lit<sub>s.</sub>

– Improvements are more pronounced for longer recommendation lists. The advantages <sub>are c</sub>l<sub>earer a</sub>t t<sub>op-</sub>20<sub>, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h b<sub>e</sub>tt<sub>er re</sub>fl<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>s ran</sub>ki<sub>ng qua</sub>lit<sub>y w</sub>h<sub>en more</sub> it<sub>ems are re</sub>t<sub>urne</sub>d<sub>.</sub> F<sub>or</sub> exam<sub>p</sub>le, com<sub>p</sub>ared to the stron<sub>g</sub>est baseline, MAGNET (Dual-view) im<sub>p</sub>roves R@20 from 0.1165 t<sub>o</sub> 0<sub>.</sub>1198 <sub>on</sub> S<sub>por</sub>t<sub>s an</sub>d f<sub>rom</sub> 0<sub>.</sub>0680 t<sub>o</sub> 0<sub>.</sub>0716 <sub>on</sub> El<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ron</sub>i<sub>cs; s</sub>i<sub>m</sub>il<sub>ar</sub>l<sub>y,</sub> it i<sub>mproves</sub> N<sub>@</sub>20 f<sub>rom</sub> 0.0466 to 0.0492 on Clothin<sub>g</sub> and from 0.0310 to 0.0329 on Electronics. These <sub>g</sub>ains indicate better top-N ordering rather than merely boosting a very short prefix.

## 4.11 Entropy-weighted routing stage analysis

In this subsection<sub>,</sub> we <sub>p</sub>rovide a mechanism-oriented anal<sub>y</sub>sis of the entro<sub>py</sub>-wei<sub>g</sub>hted routin<sub>g</sub> sta<sub>g</sub>e on Electronics. Diferent from the main experiments that emphasize final recommendation quality, we focus on how routing behavior evolves during training and why the entropy-guided stage design leads to stable s<sub>p</sub>ecialization. We track three com<sub>p</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> as<sub>p</sub>ects of routin<sub>g</sub> usin<sub>g</sub> <sub>q</sub>uantities defined earlier: s<sub>p</sub>ecialization stren<sub>g</sub>th from normalized routin<sub>g</sub> entro<sub>py</sub> (i.e., SpecScore as the com<sub>p</sub>lement of entro<sub>py</sub>), decisiveness from routin<sub>g</sub> concentration (Top3Mass, the <sub>p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> mass on the to<sub>p</sub>-3 ex<sub>p</sub>erts), and colla<sub>p</sub>se risk from load concentration (MaxLoad share, the maximum ex<sub>p</sub>ert trafic fraction).

We first examine the stage transition process (TOP LEFT). The model starts in a coverage stage, where SpecScore sta<sub>y</sub>s relativel<sub>y</sub> low and shows visible fluctuations due to mini-batch stochasticit<sub>y</sub>, which is ex<sub>p</sub>ected because the router is encoura<sub>g</sub>ed to maintain broad covera<sub>g</sub>e across ex<sub>p</sub>erts instead of committin<sub>g</sub> earl<sub>y</sub> to a small subset. As trainin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>roceeds<sub>,</sub> the entro<sub>py</sub>-derived switchin<sub>g</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>gna</sub>l <sub>approac</sub>h<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> th<sub>res</sub>h<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>;</sub> h<sub>owever,</sub> <sub>we</sub> d<sub>o</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>sw</sub>it<sub>c</sub>h <sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t <sub>cross</sub>i<sub>ng.</sub> I<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ea</sub>d<sub>,</sub> <sub>we</sub> <sub>mar</sub>k <sub>a</sub> <sub>s</sub>h<sub>or</sub>t t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>gger</sub> <sub>w</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ow</sub> <sub>as</sub> <sub>a</sub> d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on</sub> b<sub>u</sub>f<sub>er:</sub> <sub>we</sub> id<sub>en</sub>tif<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t ti<sub>me</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>smoo</sub>th<sub>e</sub>d <sub>s</sub>i<sub>gna</sub>l <sub>mee</sub>t<sub>s</sub> the switchin<sub>g</sub> condition and re<sub>q</sub>uire it to remain satisfied for � consecutive ste<sub>p</sub>s under the same <sub>smoo</sub>thi<sub>ng ru</sub>l<sub>e use</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e s</sub>t<sub>age con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>er.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> d<sub>es</sub>i<sub>gn</sub> filt<sub>ers ou</sub>t <sub>spur</sub>i<sub>ous cross</sub>i<sub>ngs cause</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> <sub>no</sub>i<sub>sy</sub> b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>es</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>rans</sub>iti<sub>on</sub> <sub>po</sub>i<sub>n</sub>t <sub>repro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ibl<sub>e.</sub> Aft<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>gger</sub> <sub>w</sub>i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ow,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>ll<sub>er</sub> enters the confidence/s<sub>p</sub>ecialization sta<sub>g</sub>e, and SpecScore rises and stabilizes at a hi<sub>g</sub>her level, indicatin<sub>g</sub> that routin<sub>g</sub> decisions become <sub>p</sub>ersistentl<sub>y</sub> more s<sub>p</sub>ecialized.

We further examine routing behavior after the transition (BOTTOM RIGHT). Focusing on the confidence sta<sub>g</sub>e<sub>,</sub> we a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ate routin<sub>g</sub> statistics at the e<sub>p</sub>och level to obtain a stable view of withinsta<sub>g</sub>e evolution. We re<sub>p</sub>ort two com<sub>p</sub>lementar<sub>y</sub> si<sub>g</sub>nals from the routin<sub>g p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> distribution: (i) normalized entro<sub>py</sub> as routin<sub>g</sub> uncertaint<sub>y</sub> (lower is better), and (ii) the to<sub>p</sub>1–to<sub>p</sub>2 <sub>p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> as a mar<sub>g</sub>in-st<sub>y</sub>le confidence measure (hi<sub>g</sub>her is better), which com<sub>p</sub>lements concentration summaries such as Top3Mass. After the switch, uncertaint<sub>y</sub> consistentl<sub>y</sub> decreases while confidence increases, indicatin<sub>g</sub> that the router <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>g</sub>ressivel<sub>y</sub> makes shar<sub>p</sub>er and more decisive ex<sub>p</sub>ert selections as t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>procee</sub>d<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>con</sub>fid<sub>ence</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>age.</sub> R<sub>es</sub>id<sub>ua</sub>l fl<sub>uc</sub>t<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>expec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d d<sub>ue</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>m</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>-</sub>b<sub>a</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h com<sub>p</sub>osition and hard exam<sub>p</sub>les<sub>,</sub> but the dominant trend is a sustained reduction in ambi<sub>g</sub>uit<sub>y</sub> accom<sub>p</sub>anied b<sub>y g</sub>rowin<sub>g</sub> se<sub>p</sub>aration between the most likel<sub>y</sub> ex<sub>p</sub>erts.

Next, TOP RIGHT provides a strategy-level diagnostic to disentangle stronger specialization f<sub>rom pa</sub>th<sub>o</sub>l<sub>og</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>co</sub>ll<sub>apse.</sub> W<sub>e compare severa</sub>l f<sub>eas</sub>ibl<sub>e rou</sub>ti<sub>ng-con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l <sub>var</sub>i<sub>an</sub>t<sub>s un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> th<sub>e same</sub> trainin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>rotocol and summarize their routin<sub>g</sub> behavior usin<sub>g</sub> three normalized statistics on a shared scale: s<sub>p</sub>ecialization stren<sub>g</sub>th (SpecScore), routin<sub>g</sub> decisiveness/concentration (Top3Mass), and load concentration risk (MaxLoad share). All three statistics are com<sub>p</sub>uted from the same underl<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> routin<sub>g</sub> out<sub>p</sub>uts and a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ated over a consistent <sub>p</sub>ost-switch window (a fixed <sub>p</sub>ortion of trainin<sub>g</sub> after the sta<sub>g</sub>e transition), which enables a fair com<sub>p</sub>arison across strate<sub>g</sub>ies. ## 5.1 Complexity and Eficiency

## 5.3 Case Study

5.3.1 How the cases are constructed. Each column in Figure 14 corresponds to a single retrieval instance from our evaluation <sub>p</sub>i<sub>p</sub>eline: a user context (recent interactions), the current <sub>q</sub>uer<sub>y</sub>/context, a labeled positive target item (GT), and the retrieved list containing a contrast item (CAND) and, when available, a confusable high-ranked negative (HARD NEG). During runs, we log the retrieved top-� list and the routing/gating weights over the 9 experts. CAND is selected from the top-� as a representative non-GT item for contrast, while HARD NEG is mined as a highly ranked negative most confusable with GT (e. ., similar surface form/attributes/visuals but incorrect b label).

We select three instances (one <sub>p</sub>er column) b<sub>y</sub> stratif<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> on the routin<sub>g</sub> si<sub>g</sub>nature (dominant famil<sub>y p</sub>attern) and enforcin<sub>g p</sub>resentation constraints: com<sub>p</sub>lete modalit<sub>y</sub>/metadata fields (no missing image/text/attribute), a clear GT–(CAND/HARD NEG) contrast within the retrieved list, <sub>an</sub>d <sub>coverage</sub> <sub>across</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t d<sub>oma</sub>i<sub>ns.</sub> All <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>t i<sub>mages</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l<sub>og</sub> th<sub>um</sub>b<sub>na</sub>il<sub>s;</sub> th<sub>ey</sub> are onl<sub>y</sub> resized/cro<sub>pp</sub>ed (and o<sub>p</sub>tionall<sub>y</sub> back<sub>g</sub>round-removed) for la<sub>y</sub>out consistenc<sub>y</sub>.

## 5.3.3 Key takeaways. From Figure 14, we draw the following conclusions:

– Balanced routing stabilizes intent while refining details. In the BALANCED case, rout i<sub>ng mass sprea</sub>d<sub>s across comp</sub>l<sub>emen</sub>t<sub>ary sources:</sub> b<sub>e</sub>h<sub>av</sub>i<sub>or anc</sub>h<sub>ors</sub> th<sub>e user</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>, an</sub>d <sub>a</sub>t<sub>-</sub> tributes/visual cues <sub>p</sub>rovide mild refinement<sub>,</sub> im<sub>p</sub>rovin<sub>g</sub> rankin<sub>g</sub> without over-committin<sub>g</sub> to a sin<sub>g</sub>le cue.

– Complementary routing resolves ambiguity and demotes confusable negatives. In the COMPLEMENTARY case, no single modality is suficient; cross-modal cues jointly disambiguate near-misses and can demote a high-ranked HARD NEG once the ambiguity is resolved.

– Dominant routing makes decisive selections under a single strong discriminator. In the DOMINANT case, a decisive attribute-level constraint drives a peaked routing distribution, leadin<sub>g</sub> to a <sub>p</sub>recise to<sub>p</sub>-1 selection<sub>,</sub> while other si<sub>g</sub>nals mainl<sub>y p</sub>la<sub>y</sub> a su<sub>pp</sub>ortin<sub>g</sub> role.

Overall<sub>,</sub> the case stud<sub>y</sub> com<sub>p</sub>lements our <sub>q</sub>uantitative results b<sub>y</sub> makin<sub>g</sub> the evidence-to-routin<sub>g</sub>- t<sub>o-ou</sub>t<sub>come c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n exp</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it <sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>ns</sub>t<sub>ance</sub> l<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>.</sub>

(i) Dual-view structural augmentation: <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ar</sub>ti<sub>n</sub> f<sub>rom co</sub>ll<sub>a</sub>b<sub>ora</sub>ti<sub>ve s</sub>t<sub>ruc</sub>t<sub>ures,</sub> it i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>uces a sma</sub>ll <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>con</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t<sub>-s</sub>i<sub>m</sub>il<sub>ar</sub>it <sub>user–</sub>it<sub>em can</sub>di d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> t<sub>o</sub> f<sub>orm an augmen</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>v</sub>i<sub>ew, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h i<sub>s enco</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n para</sub>ll<sub>e</sub>l <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erac</sub>ti<sub>on v</sub>i<sub>ew</sub> and integrated via lightweight fusion. (ii) Structured expert space for fusion: it organizes experts ex<sub>p</sub>licitl<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y</sub> modalit<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s and ex<sub>p</sub>ert families<sub>,</sub> and instantiates inter<sub>p</sub>retable fusion <sub>p</sub>atterns— dominant, balanced, and complementary—via triplet templates.
